King opposed the war because it took resources away [from social welfare at home](21109533.md): "A nation that continues year after year to spend more money on military defense than on programs of social uplift is approaching spiritual death."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins2007109-220] He stated that [North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam) "did not begin to send in any large number of supplies or men until American forces had arrived in the tens of thousands",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins2007106-221] and accused the U.S. of having killed a million Vietnamese, "mostly children".[^cite_note-222] King also criticized American opposition to North Vietnam's land reforms.[^cite_note-223]

King's opposition cost him significant support among white allies including Johnson, [Billy Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Graham), union leaders, and powerful publishers.[^cite_note-MED08-224][^cite_note-Shellnutt_2018-225][^cite_note-Blake_2018-226] "The press is being stacked against me", King said,[^cite_note-227] complaining of what he described as a double standard that applauded his nonviolence at home, but deplored it when applied "toward little brown Vietnamese children".[^cite_note-Pierre2011-228] *[Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_\(magazine\))* magazine called the speech "demagogic slander that sounded like a script for [Radio Hanoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Hanoi)",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins2007109-220] and *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* declared that King had "diminished his usefulness to his cause, his country, his people."[^cite_note-Pierre2011-228][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawsonPaynePatterson2006148-229]



King speaking to an anti-Vietnam war rally at the [University of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota) in St. Paul on April 27, 1967

The "Beyond Vietnam" speech reflected King's evolving political advocacy in his later years, which paralleled the teachings of the progressive [Highlander Research and Education Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlander_Research_and_Education_Center), with which he was affiliated.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] King began to speak of the need for fundamental changes in the American political and economic situation, and more frequently expressed his opposition to the war and his desire to see a redistribution of resources to correct injustice.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] He guarded his language in public to avoid being linked to [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), but in private sometimes spoke of his support for [democratic socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_socialism).[^cite_note-Sturm1990-234][^cite_note-235]

King stated in "Beyond Vietnam" that "true compassion is more than flinging a coin to a beggar ... it comes to see that an edifice which produces beggars needs restructuring."[^cite_note-Zinn_2002-236] King quoted a US official who said that from Vietnam to Latin America, the country was "on the wrong side of a world revolution."[^cite_note-Zinn_2002-236] King condemned America's "alliance with the landed gentry of Latin America", and said the US should support "the shirtless and barefoot people" in the [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World) rather than suppressing their attempts at revolution.[^cite_note-Zinn_2002-236]

King's stance on Vietnam encouraged [Allard K. Lowenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allard_K._Lowenstein), [William Sloane Coffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Sloane_Coffin) and [Norman Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Thomas), with the support of anti-war Democrats, to attempt to persuade King to run against President Johnson in the [1968 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_United_States_presidential_election). King contemplated but decided against this as he felt uneasy with politics and considered himself better suited to activism.[^cite_note-237]

On April 15, 1967, King spoke at an anti-war march from Manhattan's Central Park to the UN. The march was organized by the [Spring Mobilization Committee to End the War in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Mobilization_Committee_to_End_the_War_in_Vietnam) under chairman James Bevel. At the UN King brought up issues of civil rights and the draft:

> I have not urged a mechanical fusion of the civil rights and peace movements. There are people who have come to see the moral imperative of equality, but who cannot yet see the moral imperative of world brotherhood. I would like to see the fervor of the civil-rights movement imbued into the peace movement to instill it with greater strength. And I believe everyone has a duty to be in both the civil-rights and peace movements. But for those who presently choose but one, I would hope they will finally come to see the moral roots common to both.[^cite_note-238]

Seeing an opportunity to unite civil rights and anti-war activists,[^cite_note-kingandvietnam2-216] Bevel convinced King to become even more active in the anti-war effort.[^cite_note-kingandvietnam2-216] Despite his growing public opposition to the war, King was not fond of the [hippie culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_movement) developed from the anti-war movement.[^cite_note-kingandvietnam3-239] In his 1967 [Massey Lecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massey_Lectures), King stated:

> The importance of the hippies is not in their unconventional behavior, but in the fact that hundreds of thousands of young people, in turning to a flight from reality, are expressing a profoundly discrediting view on the society they emerge from.[^cite_note-kingandvietnam3-239]

On January 13, 1968, King called for a large march on Washington against "one of history's most cruel and senseless wars":[^cite_note-kurlansky2004-240][^cite_note-nyt-13jan1968-241]

> We need to make clear in this political year, to congressmen on both sides of the aisle and to the president of the United States, that we will no longer tolerate, we will no longer vote for men who continue to see the killings of Vietnamese and Americans as the best way of advancing the goals of freedom and self-determination in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-kurlansky2004-240][^cite_note-nyt-13jan1968-241]

#### Correspondence with Thích Nhất Hạnh

[Thích Nhất Hạnh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%ADch_Nh%E1%BA%A5t_H%E1%BA%A1nh) was an influential Vietnamese [Buddhist](3267529.md) who wrote a letter to King in 1965 entitled: "In Search of the Enemy of Man". It was during his 1966 stay in the US that Nhất Hạnh met with King and urged him to publicly denounce the war.[^cite_note-242] On April 4, 1967, King gave a speech in New York City, his first to publicly question U.S. involvement in Vietnam.[^cite_note-243] Later that year, King nominated Nhất Hạnh for the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md). In his nomination, King said, "I do not personally know of anyone more worthy of \[this prize] than this gentle monk from Vietnam. His ideas for peace, if applied, would build a monument to [ecumenism](146656.md), to world brotherhood, to humanity".[^cite_note-nomination-244]

### Poor People's Campaign, 1968

Main article: [Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign)

Rows of tents

A shantytown established in Washington, D.C. to protest economic conditions as a part of the [Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign)

In 1968, King and the SCLC organized the "[Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign)" to address economic justice. King traveled the country to assemble "a multiracial army of the poor" that would march on Washington to engage in nonviolent [civil disobedience](37073.md) at the Capitol until Congress created an "economic bill of rights".[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-lied-246]

The campaign was preceded by King's final book, *[Where Do We Go from Here: Chaos or Community?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Where_Do_We_Go_from_Here:_Chaos_or_Community%3F)* which laid out his view of how to address social issues and poverty. King quoted from [Henry George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_George)'s book *[Progress and Poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_and_Poverty)*, particularly in support of a [guaranteed basic income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_income).[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] The campaign culminated in a march on Washington, D.C., demanding economic aid to the poorest communities of the U.S.

King and the SCLC called on the government to invest in rebuilding America's cities. He felt that Congress had shown "hostility to the poor" by spending "military funds with alacrity and generosity". He contrasted this with the situation faced by poor Americans, claiming that Congress had merely provided "poverty funds with miserliness".[^cite_note-lied-246] His vision was for change that was more revolutionary than mere reform: he cited systematic flaws of "racism, poverty, militarism and materialism", and argued that "reconstruction of society itself is the real issue to be faced."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawsonPaynePatterson2006148–49-250]

The Poor People's Campaign was controversial even within the civil rights movement. Rustin resigned from the march, stating that the goals of the campaign were too broad, that its demands were unrealizable, and that he thought that these campaigns would accelerate repression on the poor and the black.[^cite_note-251]

### Global policy

King was one of the signatories of the agreement to convene a convention for drafting a [world constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_constitution).[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] As a result, in 1968 a [World Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Constituent_Assembly) convened to draft and adopt the [Constitution for the Federation of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_for_the_Federation_of_Earth).[^cite_note-254]

## Assassination and aftermath

Main article: [Assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.)



The Lorraine Motel, where King was assassinated, is now the site of the [National Civil Rights Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Civil_Rights_Museum).

On March 29, 1968, King went to Memphis, Tennessee, in support of the black [sanitation workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation_workers), who were represented by [American Federation of State, County and Municipal Employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Federation_of_State,_County_and_Municipal_Employees) (AFSCME) Local 1733. The workers had been [on strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_sanitation_strike) since March 12 for higher wages and better treatment. In one incident, black street repairmen received pay for two hours when they were sent home because of bad weather, but white employees were paid for the full day.[^cite_note-AFSCME-WEB1-255][^cite_note-AFSCME-WEB2-256][^cite_note-257]

On April 3, King addressed a rally and delivered his "[I've Been to the Mountaintop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I've_Been_to_the_Mountaintop)" address at [Mason Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mason_Temple). King's flight to Memphis had been delayed by a bomb threat against his plane.[^cite_note-258] In reference to the bomb threat, King said:

> And then I got to Memphis. And some began to say the threats, or talk about the threats that were out. What would happen to me from some of our sick white brothers? Well, I don't know what will happen now. We've got some difficult days ahead. But it doesn't matter with me now. Because I've been to the mountaintop. And I don't mind. Like anybody, I would like to live a long life. Longevity has its place. But I'm not concerned about that now. I just want to do God's will. And He's allowed me to go up to the mountain. And I've looked over. And I've seen the promised land. I may not get there with you. But I want you to know tonight, that we, as a people, will get to the promised land. So I'm happy, tonight. I'm not worried about anything. I'm not fearing any man. Mine eyes have seen the glory of the coming of the Lord.[^cite_note-259]

King was booked in Room 306 at the [Lorraine Motel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorraine_Motel) in Memphis. [Ralph Abernathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Abernathy), who was present at the assassination, testified to the [United States House Select Committee on Assassinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Select_Committee_on_Assassinations) that King and his entourage stayed at Room 306 so often that it was known as the "King-Abernathy suite".[^cite_note-usdoj-260] According to [Jesse Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Jackson), who was present, King's last words were spoken to musician [Ben Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Branch), who was scheduled to perform that night at an event King was attending: "Ben, make sure you play '[Take My Hand, Precious Lord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Take_My_Hand,_Precious_Lord)' in the meeting tonight. Play it real pretty."[^cite_note-261]

King was fatally shot by [James Earl Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Earl_Ray) at 6\:01 p.m., Thursday, April 4, 1968, as he stood on the motel's second-floor balcony. The bullet entered through his right cheek, smashing his jaw, then traveled down his spinal cord before lodging in his shoulder.[^cite_note-CHI-262][^cite_note-263] Abernathy heard the shot from inside the motel room and ran to the balcony to find King on the floor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrady2002204–05-264]

After emergency surgery, King died at [St. Joseph's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Joseph's_Hospital_\(Memphis,_Tennessee\)) at 7\:05 p.m.[^cite_note-265] According to biographer [Taylor Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Branch), King's [autopsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopsy) revealed that though only 39 years old, he "had the heart of a 60 year old", which Branch attributed to stress.[^cite_note-266] King was initially interred in South View Cemetery in South Atlanta, but in 1977, his remains were transferred to a tomb on the site of the [Martin Luther King Jr. National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._National_Historical_Park).[^cite_note-nhsnom-267]

### Aftermath

Further information: [King assassination riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots)

The assassination led to [race riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_racial_violence_in_the_United_States) in [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C._riots), [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Chicago_riots), [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_riot_of_1968), [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_riots_of_1968), [Kansas City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Kansas_City_riot), and dozens of other cities.[^cite_note-BBC-268][^cite_note-269][^cite_note-202004xxSmithsonianMagazineClayRisen-270] Presidential candidate [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) was on his way to [Indianapolis](57707.md) for a campaign rally when he was informed of King's death. He gave [a short, improvised speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy's_speech_on_the_assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) to the gathering of supporters informing them of the tragedy and urging them to continue King's ideal of nonviolence.[^cite_note-271] The following day, he delivered [a prepared response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Mindless_Menace_of_Violence) in Cleveland.[^cite_note-272] [James Farmer Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Farmer_Jr.) and other civil rights leaders also called for non-violent action, while the more militant [Stokely Carmichael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stokely_Carmichael) called for a more forceful response.[^cite_note-1968_Year_In_Review,_UPI.com-273] The city of Memphis quickly settled the strike on terms favorable to the sanitation workers.[^cite_note-AFSCME-WEB3-274]

The plan to set up a [shantytown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shantytown) in Washington, D.C., was carried out soon after the April 4 assassination. Criticism of King's plan was subdued in the wake of his death, and the SCLC received an unprecedented wave of donations to carry it out. The campaign officially began in Memphis, on May 2, at the hotel where King was murdered.[^cite_note-McKnight-275] Thousands of demonstrators arrived on the [National Mall](167796.md) and stayed for six weeks, establishing a camp they called "[Resurrection City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign)".[^cite_note-Engler15Jan10-276]

President Johnson tried to quell the riots by making telephone calls to civil rights leaders, mayors and governors and told politicians that they should warn the police against the unwarranted use of force.[^cite_note-202004xxSmithsonianMagazineClayRisen-270] "I'm not getting through," Johnson told his aides. "They're all holing up like generals in a dugout getting ready to watch a war."[^cite_note-202004xxSmithsonianMagazineClayRisen-270] Johnson declared April 7 a national day of mourning for King.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEManheimer200497-277] Vice President [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey) attended King's funeral on behalf of the President, as there were fears that Johnson's presence might incite protests and perhaps violence.[^cite_note-278] At his widow's request, King's last sermon at [Ebenezer Baptist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebenezer_Baptist_Church_\(Atlanta,_Georgia\)), given on February 4, 1968, was played at the funeral,

> I'd like somebody to mention that day that Martin Luther King Jr. tried to give his life serving others. I'd like for somebody to say that day that Martin Luther King Jr. tried to love somebody. I want you to say that day that I tried to be right on the war question. I want you to be able to say that day that I did try to feed the hungry. I want you to be able to say that day that I did try in my life to clothe those who were naked. I want you to say on that day that I did try in my life to visit those who were in prison. And I want you to say that I tried to love and serve humanity. Yes, if you want to say that I was a drum major. Say that I was a drum major for justice. Say that I was a drum major for peace. I was a drum major for righteousness. And all of the other shallow things will not matter. I won't have any money to leave behind. I won't have the fine and luxurious things of life to leave behind. But I just want to leave a committed life behind.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-1968_Year_In_Review,_UPI.com-273][^cite_note-280]

His friend Mahalia Jackson sang his favorite hymn, "Take My Hand, Precious Lord", at the funeral.[^cite_note-281] The assassination helped to spur the enactment of the [Civil Rights Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1968).[^cite_note-202004xxSmithsonianMagazineClayRisen-270] Two months after King's death, [James Earl Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Earl_Ray)—on the loose from a previous prison escape—was captured at [London Heathrow Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Heathrow_Airport) while trying to reach white-ruled [Rhodesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodesia) on a false Canadian passport. He was using the alias Ramon George Sneyd.[^cite_note-282] Ray was quickly extradited to Tennessee and charged with King's murder. He confessed on March 10, 1969, though he recanted this confession three days later.[^cite_note-extradite-283] On the advice of his attorney [Percy Foreman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Foreman), Ray pleaded guilty to avoid the possibility of the death penalty. He was sentenced to a 99-year prison term.[^cite_note-extradite-283][^cite_note-cbs-284] Ray later claimed a man he met in [Montreal](7954681.md), Quebec, with the alias "Raoul" was involved and that the assassination was the result of a conspiracy.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-davis-286] He spent the remainder of his life attempting, unsuccessfully, to withdraw his guilty plea and secure the trial he never had.[^cite_note-cbs-284] Ray died in 1998 at age 70.[^cite_note-287]

### Allegations of conspiracy

Main article: [Martin Luther King Jr. assassination conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._assassination_conspiracy_theories)



The [sarcophagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarcophagus) for Martin Luther King Jr. and Coretta Scott King is within the [Martin Luther King Jr. National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._National_Historical_Park) in [Atlanta](3138.md), Georgia.

Ray's lawyers maintained he was a [scapegoat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapegoating) similar to the way that John F. Kennedy's assassin [Lee Harvey Oswald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Harvey_Oswald) is seen by [conspiracy theorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination_conspiracy_theories).[^cite_note-CNN1-288] Supporters of this assertion said that Ray's confession was given under pressure and that he had been threatened with the death penalty.[^cite_note-cbs-284][^cite_note-289] They admitted that Ray was a thief and burglar, but claimed that he had no record of committing violent crimes with a weapon.[^cite_note-davis-286] However, prison records in different U.S. cities have shown that he was incarcerated on numerous occasions for armed robbery.[^cite_note-mlkassassin-290] In a 2008 interview with [CNN](62028.md), Jerry Ray, the younger brother of James Earl Ray, claimed that James was smart and was sometimes able to get away with armed robbery. "I never been with nobody as bold as he is," Jerry said. "He just walked in and put that gun on somebody, it was just like it's an everyday thing."[^cite_note-mlkassassin-290]

Those suspecting a conspiracy point to the two successive [ballistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistics) tests which proved that a rifle similar to Ray's [Remington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remington_Arms) Gamemaster had been the murder weapon. Those tests did not implicate Ray's specific rifle.[^cite_note-cbs-284][^cite_note-BBC-WEB1-291] Witnesses near King said that the shot came from another location, from behind thick shrubbery near the boarding house—which had been cut away in the days following the assassination—and not from the boarding house window.[^cite_note-Gerold-292] However, Ray's fingerprints were found on various objects in the bathroom where it was determined the gunfire came from.[^cite_note-mlkassassin-290] An examination of the rifle containing Ray's fingerprints determined that at least one shot was fired from the firearm at the time of the assassination.[^cite_note-mlkassassin-290]

In 1997, King's son Dexter Scott King met with Ray, and publicly supported Ray's efforts to obtain a [new trial](3264468.md).[^cite_note-CNN2-293] Two years later, King's widow Coretta Scott King and the couple's children, represented by [William F. Pepper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_F._Pepper),[^cite_note-294] won a [wrongful death claim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrongful_death_claim) against [Loyd Jowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyd_Jowers) and "other unknown co-conspirators". Jowers claimed to have received \$100,000 to arrange King's assassination. The jury found Jowers to be complicit in a conspiracy and that government agencies were party to the assassination.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]&#x20;

In 2000, the [U.S. Department of Justice](52563.md) completed the investigation into Jowers' claims but did not find evidence of conspiracy. The investigation report recommended no further investigation unless new reliable facts are presented.[^cite_note-usdoj2-297] A sister of Jowers admitted that he had fabricated the story so he could make \$300,000 from selling the story, and she corroborated his story to get money to pay her income tax.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]

In 2002, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that a church minister, Ronald Denton Wilson, claimed his father, Henry Clay Wilson, assassinated King. He stated, "It wasn't a racist thing; he thought Martin Luther King was connected with communism, and he wanted to get him out of the way." Wilson provided no evidence to back up his claims.[^cite_note-NYTORIG-300]

King researchers [David Garrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Garrow) and [Gerald Posner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Posner) disagreed with Pepper's claims that the government killed King.[^cite_note-301] In 2003, Pepper published a book about the investigation and trial, as well as his representation of James Earl Ray in his bid for a trial.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] James Bevel also disputed the argument that Ray acted alone, stating, "There is no way a ten-cent white boy could develop a plan to kill a million-dollar black man."[^cite_note-304] In 2004, Jesse Jackson stated:[^cite_note-Demo-305]

> The fact is there were saboteurs to disrupt the march. And within our own organization, we found a very key person who was on the government payroll. So infiltration within, saboteurs from without and the press attacks. ... I will never believe that James Earl Ray had the motive, the money and the mobility to have done it himself. Our government was very involved in setting the stage for and I think the escape route for James Earl Ray.

On January 23, 2025, [President Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_Donald_Trump) signed an Executive Order declassifying the records concerning the assassination.[^cite_note-306]

## Legacy

See also: [Memorials to Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorials_to_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) and [List of streets named after Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_streets_named_after_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.)



Martin Luther King Jr. statue over the west entrance of [Westminster Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Abbey), installed in 1998



Martin Luther King Jr Blvd, in [Chapel Hill, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapel_Hill,_North_Carolina)



Martin Luther King Jnr. at The Heroes Square in [Owerri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owerri), [Imo State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imo_State)

### South Africa

See also: [Black Consciousness Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Consciousness_Movement)

King's legacy includes influences on the [Black Consciousness Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Consciousness_Movement) and civil rights movement in South Africa.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] King's work was cited by, and served as, an inspiration for South African leader [Albert Luthuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Luthuli), who fought for racial justice in his country during [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) and was later awarded the Nobel Peace Prize.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing1992307–308-309]

### United Kingdom

See also: [Northern Ireland civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_civil_rights_movement)

[John Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hume), the former leader of the [Social Democratic and Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_and_Labour_Party), cited King's legacy as quintessential to the [Northern Ireland civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_civil_rights_movement) and the signing of the [Good Friday Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement), calling him "one of my great heroes of the century".[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312]

The Martin Luther King Fund and Foundation in the UK was set up as a charity[^cite_note-\:1-313] on December 30, 1969, after King's assassination and following a visit to the UK in 1969 by his widow, [Coretta King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coretta_Scott_King). The Foundation's first chairman, Canon [John Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Collins_\(priest\)), stated that the Foundation was to be an active UK national campaign for racial equality, its work also to include community projects in areas of social need, and education.[^cite_note-\:3-314] International Personnel (IP), an employment agency, was formed in 1970 out of the foundation's base in [Balham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balham), to find employment for professionally qualified black people. In its first year, the agency placed ten percent of its applicants in jobs equal to their ability.[^cite_note-\:4-315] The Balham Training Scheme operated an evening school with lecturers in Typing, Shorthand, English and Math.[^cite_note-\:3-314] The foundation was removed from the Charity Commission list on November 18, 1996, as it had ceased to exist.[^cite_note-\:1-313] The Northumbria and Newcastle Universities Martin Luther King Peace Committee[^cite_note-316] still exists to honor King's legacy, as represented by his final visit to the UK to receive an honorary degree from [Newcastle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_University) in 1967.[^cite_note-Newcastle_ceremony-317][^cite_note-Ward-318] Northumbria and Newcastle remain centers for the study of Martin Luther King and the US civil rights movement. Inspired by King's vision, the committee undertakes a range of activities across the UK to "build cultures of peace".

In 2017, Newcastle University unveiled a bronze statue of King to celebrate the 50th anniversary of his honorary doctorate ceremony.[^cite_note-319] The Students Union also voted to rename their bar "Luther's".[^cite_note-320]

### United States

King has become an icon in the history of [American liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States) and [American progressivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_progressivism).[^cite_note-321] His main legacy was to secure progress on civil rights in the U.S. Just days after King's assassination, Congress passed the [Civil Rights Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1968).[^cite_note-HUDHistory-322] Title VIII of the Act, commonly known as the Fair Housing Act, prohibited discrimination in housing and housing-related transactions on the basis of race, religion, or national origin (later expanded to include sex, familial status, and disability). This legislation was seen as a tribute to King's struggle in his final years to combat residential discrimination.[^cite_note-HUDHistory-322] The day following King's assassination, teacher [Jane Elliott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Elliott) conducted her first "Blue Eyes/Brown Eyes" exercise with her class of elementary school students to help them understand King's death as it related to racism.[^cite_note-323]

King's wife Coretta Scott King was active in matters of social justice and civil rights until her death in 2006. The same year that King was assassinated, she established the [King Center in Atlanta, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Center_for_Nonviolent_Social_Change), dedicated to preserving his legacy and the work of championing nonviolent conflict resolution and tolerance worldwide.[^cite_note-KC-WEB1-324] Their son, Dexter King, who died in 2024, served as the center's chairman until 2010.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] In 2010, [Martin Luther King III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_III) became president. In 2012, King's youngest child, [Bernice King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernice_King), became the CEO. Daughter Yolanda King, who died in 2007, was a motivational speaker, author and founder of Higher Ground Productions, an organization specializing in diversity training.[^cite_note-327]

King family members disagree about his views about [LGBTQ](78174325.md) people. King's widow Coretta said that she believed her husband would have supported them.[^cite_note-328] However, King's youngest child, Bernice, said that he would have been opposed to [gay marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_marriage).[^cite_note-MPR-WEB1-329] King himself, in a 1958 advice column written for [*Ebony*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebony_\(magazine\)), called homosexuality a "problem" and suggested that it could be resolved through [psychiatric treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychotherapy).[^cite_note-\:6-330]

#### Martin Luther King Jr. Day

Main article: [Martin Luther King Jr. Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Day)

Beginning in 1971, cities and states established annual holidays to honor King.[^cite_note-stlouis-331] On November 2, 1983, President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) signed a bill creating a federal holiday to honor King. Observed for the first time on January 20, 1986, it is called [Martin Luther King Jr. Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Day). Following President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush)'s 1992 proclamation, the holiday is observed on the third Monday of January each year, near the time of King's birthday.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] On January 17, 2000, for the first time, Martin Luther King Jr. Day was officially observed in all fifty U.S. states.[^cite_note-334] [Arizona](21883824.md) (1992), [New Hampshire](21134.md) (1999) and [Utah](31716.md) (2000) were the last states to recognize the holiday. Utah previously celebrated the holiday under the name Human Rights Day.[^cite_note-335]

## Veneration

| Martin Luther King of Georgia |  |
| --- | --- |
| Pastor and Martyr |  |
| Honored in | Holy Christian Orthodox Church [Episcopal Church (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)) [Evangelical Lutheran Church in America](248255.md) |
| [Canonized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization) | September 9, 2016, The Christian Cathedral by Timothy Paul Baymon |
| [Feast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_of_saints) | April 4 January 15 (Episcopalian and Lutheran) |

King was [canonized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization) by Archbishop [Timothy Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Paul_Baymon) of the Holy Christian Orthodox Church on September 9, 2016.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] His feast day was set as April 4, the date of his assassination. King is also honored with a Lesser Feast on the [liturgical calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_\(Episcopal_Church\)) of the [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\))[^cite_note-341] on April 4 or January 15, the anniversary of his birth. The [Evangelical Lutheran Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_of_Saints_\(Lutheran\)) commemorates King liturgically on January 15.[^cite_note-342]

## Ideas, influences, and political stances

### Christianity

As a Christian minister, King's main influence was [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) and the Christian gospels, which he frequently cited in his speeches.[^cite_note-343] King's faith was strongly based in the [Golden Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Rule), loving God above all, and loving your enemies. His [nonviolent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolence) thought was also based in the injunction to *[turn the other cheek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turn_the_other_cheek)* in the [Sermon on the Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount), and Jesus' teaching of putting the sword back into its place (Matthew 26\:52).[^cite_note-344] In his [Letter from Birmingham Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letter_from_Birmingham_Jail), King urged action consistent with what he describes as Jesus' "extremist" love, and also quoted numerous other [Christian pacifist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pacifism) authors. In another sermon, he stated:

> Before I was a civil rights leader, I was a preacher of the Gospel. This was my first calling and it still remains my greatest commitment. You know, actually all that I do in civil rights I do because I consider it a part of my ministry. I have no other ambitions in life but to achieve excellence in the Christian ministry. I don't plan to run for any political office. I don't plan to do anything but remain a preacher. And what I'm doing in this struggle, along with many others, grows out of my feeling that the preacher must be concerned about the whole man.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346]

King's private writings as an undergraduate in seminary show that he rejected [biblical literalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_literalism); he described the Bible as "[mythological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mythology)", doubted that Jesus was [born of a virgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_birth_of_Jesus) and did not believe that the [story of Jonah and the whale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Jonah) was true.[^cite_note-347]

Among the thinkers who influenced King's theological outlook were [L. Harold DeWolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Harold_DeWolf), [Edgar Brightman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Brightman), [Peter Bertocci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Bertocci), [Walter George Muelder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_George_Muelder), [Walter Rauschenbusch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rauschenbusch), and [Reinhold Niebuhr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Niebuhr).[^cite_note-Ansbro-348]

#### *The Measure of a Man*

In 1959, King published a short book called *The Measure of a Man*, which contained his sermons "[What is Man?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/What_Is_Man%3F_\(King_essay\))" and "The Dimensions of a Complete Life". The sermons argued for man's need for God's love and criticized the racial injustices of Western civilization.[^cite_note-349]

### Nonviolence

A close-up of Rustin

King worked alongside Quakers such as [Bayard Rustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayard_Rustin) to develop nonviolent tactics.

> World peace through nonviolent means is neither absurd nor unattainable. All other methods have failed. Thus we must begin anew. Nonviolence is a good starting point. Those of us who believe in this method can be voices of reason, sanity, and understanding amid the voices of violence, hatred, and emotion. We can very well set a mood of peace out of which a system of peace can be built.

— Martin Luther King Jr.

[^cite_note-Amherst-350]

African-American civil rights activist [Bayard Rustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayard_Rustin) was King's first regular advisor on [nonviolence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolence).[^cite_note-351] King was also advised by the white activists [Harris Wofford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harris_Wofford) and [Glenn Smiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenn_Smiley).[^cite_note-wofford-352] Rustin and Smiley came from the [Christian pacifist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pacifist) tradition, and Wofford and Rustin both studied [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi)'s teachings. Rustin had applied nonviolence with the [Journey of Reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journey_of_Reconciliation) campaign in the 1940s,[^cite_note-353] and Wofford had been promoting [Gandhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhism) to Southern blacks since the early 1950s.[^cite_note-wofford-352]

King initially knew little about Gandhi and rarely used the term "nonviolence" during his early activism. King initially believed in and practiced self-defense, even obtaining guns to defend against possible attackers. The pacifists showed him the alternative of [nonviolent resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolent_revolution), arguing that this would be a better means to accomplish his goals. King then vowed to no longer personally use arms.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]



King at the 1963 Civil Rights March in Washington, D.C.

In a chapter of *[Stride Toward Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stride_Toward_Freedom)*, King outlined his understanding of nonviolence, which seeks to win an opponent to friendship, rather than to humiliate or defeat him. The chapter draws from an address by Wofford, with Rustin and [Stanley Levison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Levison) also providing guidance and ghostwriting.[^cite_note-356]

King was inspired by Gandhi and his success with nonviolent activism, and as a theology student, King described Gandhi as being one of the "individuals who greatly reveal the working of the Spirit of God".[^cite_note-357] King had "for a long time ... wanted to take a trip to India."[^cite_note-358] With assistance from Harris Wofford, the [American Friends Service Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Friends_Service_Committee), and other supporters, [he was able to fund the journey in February 1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959_visit_by_Martin_Luther_King_Jr._to_India).[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing199213-360] The trip deepened his understanding of [nonviolent resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolent_resistance) and his commitment to America's struggle for civil rights. In a radio address made during his final evening in India, King reflected, "Since being in India, I am more convinced than ever before that the method of nonviolent resistance is the most potent weapon available to oppressed people in their struggle for justice and human dignity."

When receiving the Nobel Peace Prize in 1964, King hailed the "successful precedent" of using nonviolence "by Mohandas K. Gandhi to challenge the might of the British Empire ... He struggled only with the weapons of truth, soul force, non-injury and courage."[^cite_note-361] Another influence was [Henry David Thoreau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_David_Thoreau)'s essay *[On Civil Disobedience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Disobedience_\(Thoreau\))* and its theme of refusing to cooperate with an evil system.[^cite_note-362] He also was greatly influenced by the works of Protestant theologians [Reinhold Niebuhr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinhold_Niebuhr) and [Paul Tillich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Tillich),[^cite_note-363] and said that [Walter Rauschenbusch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rauschenbusch)'s *Christianity and the Social Crisis* left an "indelible imprint" on his thinking by giving him a theological grounding for his social concerns.[^cite_note-Ansbro_1982_p._163-364][^cite_note-Baldwin_Burrow_Fairclough_2013_p._133-365] King was moved by Rauschenbusch's vision of Christians spreading social unrest in "perpetual but friendly conflict" with the state, simultaneously critiquing it and calling it to act as an instrument of justice.[^cite_note-Long_2002_p._53-366] However, he was apparently unaware of the [American tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism_in_the_United_States) of [Christian pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pacifism) exemplified by [Adin Ballou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adin_Ballou) and [William Lloyd Garrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lloyd_Garrison).[^cite_note-Perry_1973_p._4-367] King frequently referred to Jesus' [Sermon on the Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount) as central for his work.[^cite_note-Baldwin_Burrow_Fairclough_2013_p._133-365][^cite_note-Burrow_2014_p._313-368][^cite_note-Deats_Lenker_Perry_2004_p._37-369][^cite_note-Stott_2004_p._149-370] Before 1960, King also sometimes used the concept of "[agape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agape)" (brotherly Christian love).[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]

Even after renouncing personal use of guns, King had a complex relationship with self-defense in the movement. He publicly discouraged it as a widespread practice but acknowledged that it was sometimes necessary.[^cite_note-373] Throughout his career King was frequently protected by other civil rights activists who carried arms, such as [Colonel Stone Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonel_Stone_Johnson),[^cite_note-374] [Robert Hayling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine_Movement), and the [Deacons for Defense and Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deacons_for_Defense_and_Justice).[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

Amid recurring [riots in the mid-1960s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto_riots_\(1964%E2%80%931969\)), King reaffirmed his commitment to nonviolence. He stated that riots were "the language of the unheard", the result of a failure to improve economic conditions and address racial injustice, but condemned them as "self-defeating and socially destructive".[^cite_note-\:02-377][^cite_note-\:12-378]

### Criticism within the movement

King was criticized by other black leaders in the civil rights movement. This included more militant thinkers such as [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam_\(religious_movement\)) member [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X).[^cite_note-379] [Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee) founder [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker) regarded King as a charismatic [media figure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_authority) who lost touch with the grassroots of the movement[^cite_note-380] as he became close to elite figures like [Nelson Rockefeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Rockefeller).[^cite_note-381] [Stokely Carmichael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stokely_Carmichael), a protege of Baker's, became a black [separatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_separatism) and disagreed with King's plea for [racial integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_integration) because he considered it an insult to a uniquely [African-American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_culture).[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383] He also took issue that King's non-violence approach depended on appealing to America's conscience, feeling America had none to appeal to.[^cite_note-blackpower-384]

### Activism and involvement with Native Americans

King was an avid supporter of Native American rights and Native Americans were active supporters of King's [civil rights movement](49001.md).[^cite_note-385] The [Native American Rights Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Rights_Fund) (NARF) was patterned after the NAACP's Legal Defense and Education Fund.[^cite_note-kingcreek-386] The National Indian Youth Council (NIYC) was especially supportive in King's campaigns especially the [Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign) in 1968.[^cite_note-scielo.org.za-387] In King's book *[Why We Can't Wait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_We_Can't_Wait)* he writes:

> Our nation was born in genocide when it embraced the doctrine that the original American, the Indian, was an inferior race. Even before there were large numbers of Negroes on our shores, the scar of racial hatred had already disfigured colonial society. From the sixteenth century forward, blood flowed in battles over racial supremacy. We are perhaps the only nation which tried as a matter of national policy to wipe out its indigenous population. Moreover, we elevated that tragic experience into a noble crusade. Indeed, even today we have not permitted ourselves to reject or to feel remorse for this shameful episode. Our literature, our films, our drama, our folklore all exalt it.[^cite_note-kingnatspeech-388]

In the late 1950s, the remaining [Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creek_tribe) in Alabama were trying to completely desegregate schools. Light-complexioned Native children were allowed to ride buses to previously all-white schools, while dark-skinned Native children from the same band were barred from the same buses.[^cite_note-kingcreek-386] Tribal leaders, hearing of King's desegregation campaign in Birmingham, contacted him for assistance. Through his intervention the problem was quickly resolved.[^cite_note-kingcreek-386]

In September 1959, after giving a speech at the [University of Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Arizona) on the ideals of using nonviolent methods in creating social change, King stated his belief that one must not use force in this struggle "but match the violence of his opponents with his suffering."[^cite_note-kingindrez-389] King then went to Southside Presbyterian, a predominantly Native American church, and was fascinated by their photos; he wanted to go to an Indian Reservation to meet the people so Casper Glenn took King to the Papago Indian Reservation.[^cite_note-kingindrez-389] He met with all the tribal leaders, visited another Presbyterian church near the reservation, and preached there, attracting a Native American crowd.[^cite_note-kingindrez-389] He later returned to Old Pueblo in March 1962 where he preached again to a Native American congregation.[^cite_note-kingindrez-389] King would continue to attract the attention of Native Americans throughout the civil rights movement. During the [1963 March on Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom) there was a sizable Native American contingent, including many from South Dakota and from the [Navajo nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_nation).[^cite_note-kingcreek-386][^cite_note-navtimes-390]

King was a major inspiration, along with the [civil rights movement](49001.md), of the [Native American rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_rights_movement) of the 1960s and many of its leaders.[^cite_note-kingcreek-386] John Echohawk, a member of the [Pawnee tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pawnee_people) who was the executive director and a founder of the Native American Rights Fund, stated:

> Inspired by Dr. King, who was advancing the civil rights agenda of equality under the laws of this country, we thought that we could also use the laws to advance our Indianship, to live as tribes in our territories governed by our own laws under the principles of tribal sovereignty that had been with us ever since 1831. We believed that we could fight for a policy of self-determination that was consistent with U.S. law and that we could govern our own affairs, define our own ways and continue to survive in this society.[^cite_note-amiss-391]

### Politics

As the leader of the SCLC, King maintained a policy of not publicly endorsing a U.S. political party or candidate: "I feel someone must remain in the position of non-alignment, so that he can look objectively at both parties and be the conscience of both—not the servant or master of either."[^cite_note-Oates1993-392] In a 1958 interview, he expressed his view that neither party was perfect, saying, "I don't think the [Republican party](32070.md) is a party full of the almighty God nor is the [Democratic party](5043544.md). They both have weaknesses ... And I'm not inextricably bound to either party."[^cite_note-King-Carson2000p364-393] King did praise Democratic Senator [Paul Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Douglas_\(Illinois_politician\)) of Illinois as being the "greatest of all senators" because of his fierce advocacy for civil rights causes.[^cite_note-394]

King critiqued both parties' performance on promoting racial equality:

> Actually, the Negro has been betrayed by both the Republican and the Democratic party. The Democrats have betrayed him by capitulating to the whims and caprices of the Southern [Dixiecrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixiecrats). The Republicans have betrayed him by capitulating to the blatant hypocrisy of [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) right-wing northern Republicans. And this [coalition of southern Dixiecrats and right-wing reactionary northern Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_coalition) defeats every bill and every move towards liberal legislation in the area of civil rights.[^cite_note-King-Carson2000p84-395]

Although King never publicly supported a political party or candidate for president, in a letter to a civil rights supporter in October 1956 he said that he had not decided whether he would vote for Democrat [Adlai Stevenson II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adlai_Stevenson_II) or Republican [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) at the [1956 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_United_States_presidential_election), but that "In the past, I always voted the Democratic ticket."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing1992384-396] In his autobiography, King says that in [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election) he privately voted for Democratic candidate [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy): "I felt that Kennedy would make the best president. I never came out with an endorsement. My father did, but I never made one." King adds that he likely would have made an exception to his non-endorsement policy for a second Kennedy term, saying "Had President Kennedy lived, I would probably have endorsed him in 1964."[^cite_note-King-Carson1998p187-397]

In [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election), King urged his supporters "and all people of goodwill" to vote against Republican Senator [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater) for president, saying that his election "would be a tragedy, and certainly suicidal almost, for the nation and the world."[^cite_note-398] King believed [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) would make for a good president, but also believed that he wouldn't beat Johnson in the 1968 Democratic Party presidential primaries. He also expressed support for the possible presidential candidacies of Republicans [Nelson Rockefeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Rockefeller), [George Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Romney) and [Charles Percy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_H._Percy).[^cite_note-399]



Banner at the [2012 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Republican_National_Convention) (King's portrait blurred due to copyright)

King rejected both [*laissez-faire* capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire) and [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism); King had read [Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx) while at Morehouse but rejected communism because of its "[materialistic interpretation of history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_materialism)" that denied religion, its "[ethical relativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_relativism)", and its "[political totalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism)". He stated that one focused too much on the individual while [the other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) focused too much on the collective.[^cite_note-400]

In a 1952 letter to Coretta Scott, he said: "I imagine you already know that I am much more socialistic in my economic theory than capitalistic ..."[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-AntiCapitalism-402] In one speech, he stated that "something is wrong with capitalism" and said, "There must be a better distribution of wealth, and maybe America must move toward a democratic socialism."[^cite_note-403] King further said that "capitalism has outlived its usefulness" and "failed to meet the needs of the masses".[^cite_note-404]

King was critical of American culture saying "when machines and computers, profit motives and property rights are considered more important than people, the giant triplets of racism, materialism and militarism are incapable of being conquered" and that America must undergo a "radical revolution of values".[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] King considered that in America "the problem is that we all too often have [socialism for the rich and rugged free enterprise capitalism for the poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_for_the_rich_and_capitalism_for_the_poor)".[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408] While supporting the [Memphis sanitation strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_sanitation_strike) he told a reporter that "in a sense you could say we are engaged in the [class struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_struggle)."[^cite_note-409]

### Compensation

See also: [Reparations for slavery debate in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reparations_for_slavery_debate_in_the_United_States)

King stated that black Americans, as well as other disadvantaged Americans, should be compensated for historical wrongs. In an interview conducted for *[Playboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playboy)* in 1965, he said that granting black Americans only equality could not realistically close the economic gap between them and whites. King said that he did not seek a full restitution of wages lost to slavery, which he believed impossible, but proposed a government compensatory program of \$50 billion over ten years to all disadvantaged groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWashington1991366-410]

He posited that "the money spent would be more than amply justified by the benefits that would accrue to the nation through a spectacular decline in school dropouts, family breakups, crime rates, illegitimacy, swollen relief rolls, rioting and other social evils."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWashington1991365–67-411] He presented this idea as an application of the [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) regarding settlement of unpaid labor but clarified that he felt that the money should not be spent exclusively on blacks. He stated, "It should benefit the disadvantaged of *all* races."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWashington1991367–68-412]

### Television

&#x20;Actress [Nichelle Nichols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nichelle_Nichols) planned to leave the science-fiction television series *[Star Trek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Trek:_The_Original_Series)* in 1967 after [its first season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Trek:_The_Original_Series_\(season_1\)).[^cite_note-nprcode-413] She changed her mind after talking to King,[^cite_note-25th-414] who was a fan of the show. King explained that her character signified a future of greater racial cooperation.[^cite_note-415] King told Nichols, "You are our image of where we're going, you're 300 years from now, and that means that's where we are and it takes place now. Keep doing what you're doing, you are our inspiration."[^cite_note-416] As Nichols recounted:&#x20;

> *Star Trek* was one of the only shows that \[King] and his wife [Coretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coretta_Scott_King) would allow their little children to watch. And I thanked him and I told him I was leaving the show. All the smile came off his face. And he said, 'Don't you understand for the first time we're seen as we should be seen. You don't have a black role. You have an equal role.'[^cite_note-nprcode-413]

&#x20;The series' creator, [Gene Roddenberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Roddenberry), was deeply moved upon learning of King's support.[^cite_note-417]

## State surveillance and coercion

### FBI surveillance and wiretapping



Memo describing FBI attempts to disrupt the Poor People's Campaign with fraudulent claims about King‍—‌part of the [COINTELPRO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COINTELPRO) campaign against the anti-war and civil rights movements

FBI director [J. Edgar Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover) personally ordered surveillance of King, with the intent to undermine his power as a civil rights leader.[^cite_note-MED08-2-418][^cite_note-Honey2007ch4-419] The [Church Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Committee), a 1975 investigation by the [U.S. Congress](31756.md), found that "From December 1963 until his death in 1968, Martin Luther King Jr. was the target of an intensive campaign by the Federal Bureau of Investigation to 'neutralize' him as an effective civil rights leader."[^cite_note-Church-420]

In the fall of 1963, the FBI received authorization from Attorney General [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) to proceed with wiretapping of King's phone lines, purportedly due to his association with [Stanley Levison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Levison).[^cite_note-the_atlantic-421] The Bureau informed President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy). He and his brother unsuccessfully tried to persuade King to dissociate himself from Levison, a New York lawyer who had been involved with Communist Party USA.[^cite_note-right-422][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKotz2005-423] Although Robert Kennedy only gave written approval for limited wiretapping of King's telephone lines "on a trial basis, for a month or so",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerst2007372-424] Hoover extended the clearance so his men were "unshackled" to look for evidence in any areas of King's life they deemed worthy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerst2007372–74-120]

The Bureau placed wiretaps on the home and office phone lines of both Levison and King, and bugged King's rooms in hotels as he traveled across the country.[^cite_note-right-422][^cite_note-track-425] In 1967, Hoover listed the [SCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Christian_Leadership_Conference) as a black nationalist hate group, with the instructions: "No opportunity should be missed to exploit through counterintelligence techniques the organizational and personal conflicts of the leaderships of the groups ... to insure  \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*] the targeted group is disrupted, ridiculed, or discredited."[^cite_note-Honey2007ch4-419][^cite_note-426]

### Police surveillance

King was also the subject of extensive surveillance by local police agencies throughout the United States. At the March on Washington, where King declared, "We can never be satisfied as long as the Negro is the victim of the unspeakable horrors of police brutality", undercover police from both the Birmingham Police Department and the Philadelphia Police Department were on hand to monitor the day's proceedings. Additional documented instances of local police that monitored King include the New York Police Department and the Chicago Police Department.[^cite_note-427]

The Memphis Police Department also spied on King in the spring of 1968, as the civil rights leader was taking part in a campaign to support striking sanitation workers in the Tennessee city. A fire station was located across from the Lorraine Motel, next to the boarding house in which James Earl Ray was staying. Police officers were stationed in the fire station to keep King under surveillance.[^cite_note-428] Agents were watching King at the time he was shot.[^cite_note-429] Immediately following the shooting, officers rushed to the motel. Marrell McCollough, an undercover police officer, was the first person to administer first aid to King.[^cite_note-430] The antagonism between King and the FBI, the lack of an [all points bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_points_bulletin) to find the killer, and the police presence nearby led to speculation that the FBI was involved in the assassination.[^cite_note-431]

### NSA monitoring of King's communications

In a secret operation code-named "[Minaret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_MINARET)", the [National Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) monitored the communications of leading Americans, including King, who were critical of the [U.S. war in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_the_United_States_in_the_Vietnam_War).[^cite_note-theguardian.com-432] A review by the NSA itself concluded that Minaret was "disreputable if not outright illegal".[^cite_note-theguardian.com-432]

### Allegations of communism

For years, Hoover had been suspicious of potential [influence of communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Scare) in social movements such as labor unions and civil rights.[^cite_note-433] Hoover directed the FBI to track King in 1957, and the SCLC when it was established.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-3]

Due to the relationship between King and Stanley Levison, the FBI feared Levison was working as an "agent of influence" over King, in spite of its own reports in 1963 that Levison had left the Party and was no longer associated in business dealings with them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKotz200570–74-434] Another King lieutenant, [Jack O'Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_O'Dell), was also linked to the Communist Party by sworn testimony before the [House Un-American Activities Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Un-American_Activities_Committee) (HUAC).[^cite_note-435]

Despite the extensive surveillance, by 1976 the FBI had acknowledged that it had not obtained any evidence that King himself or the SCLC were actually involved with any communist organizations.[^cite_note-Church-420]

For his part, King adamantly denied having any connections to communism. In a 1965 *Playboy* interview, he stated that "there are as many Communists in this freedom movement as there are Eskimos in Florida."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWashington1991362-436] He argued that Hoover was "following the path of appeasement of political powers in the South" and that his concern for communist infiltration of the civil rights movement was meant to "aid and abet the salacious claims of southern racists and the extreme right-wing elements."[^cite_note-Church-420] Hoover replied by saying that King was "the most notorious liar in the country".[^cite_note-437] After his "I Have A Dream" speech, the FBI described King as "the most dangerous and effective Negro leader in the country".[^cite_note-track-425] It alleged that he was "knowingly, willingly and regularly cooperating with and taking guidance from communists."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKotz200583-438]

The attempts to prove that King was a communist were related to the feeling of many segregationists that blacks in the South were content with the status quo but had been stirred up by "communists" and "outside agitators".[^cite_note-439] King said that "the Negro revolution is a genuine revolution, born from the same womb that produces all massive social upheavals—the womb of intolerable conditions and unendurable situations."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWashington1991363-440]

### CIA surveillance

CIA files declassified in 2017 revealed that the agency was investigating possible links between King and Communism after a *Washington Post* article dated November 4, 1964, claimed he was invited to the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and that Ralph Abernathy, as spokesman for King, refused to comment on the source of the invitation.[^cite_note-441] Mail belonging to King and other civil rights activists was intercepted by the CIA program [HTLINGUAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTLINGUAL).[^cite_note-442]

### Allegations of adultery



The only meeting of King and [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X), outside the [United States Senate chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_chamber), March 26, 1964, during the Senate debates regarding the (eventual) [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964)

[^cite_note-WPKingX-443]

The FBI attempted to discredit King through revelations regarding his private life. FBI surveillance, some of it since made public, attempted to demonstrate that he had extramarital affairs.[^cite_note-track-425] The FBI distributed reports regarding such affairs to the executive branch, friendly reporters, potential coalition partners and funding sources of the SCLC, and King's family.[^cite_note-444] The bureau sent anonymous letters to King threatening to reveal information about his affairs.[^cite_note-445] The [FBI–King letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI%E2%80%93King_letter) sent to King just before he received the Nobel Peace Prize read, in part:



Part of the [FBI–King letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI%E2%80%93King_letter),

[^cite_note-fbi_letter-446]

&#x20;mailed anonymously by the FBI. Other portions of the letter which were previously not made public would be uncovered in 2014.

[^cite_note-fbi_letter-446]

> The American public, the church organizations that have been helping—Protestants, Catholics and Jews will know you for what you are—an evil beast. So will others who have backed you. You are done. King, there is only one thing left for you to do. You know what it is. You have just 34 days in which to do (this exact number has been selected for a specific reason, it has definite practical significant  \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*]). You are done. There is but one way out for you. You better take it before your filthy fraudulent self is bared to the nation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKotz2005247-447]

The letter was accompanied by a tape recording—excerpted from FBI wiretaps—of several of King's extramarital liaisons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrady2002158–159-448] King interpreted this package as an attempt to drive him to suicide,[^cite_note-449] although [William Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_C._Sullivan), then head of the Domestic Intelligence Division, argued it may have only been intended to "convince Dr. King to resign from the SCLC."[^cite_note-Church-420] Upon the release of the full letter in 2014, Yale history professor [Beverly Gage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beverly_Gage) noted in a *New York Times* article that the claim that the FBI "simply meant to push King out, not induce suicide" was a possibility, pointing out that "Another uncovered portion of the note praises "older leaders" like the NAACP executive director [Roy Wilkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Wilkins), urging King to step aside and let other men lead the civil rights movement."[^cite_note-fbi_letter-446] King refused to succumb to the FBI's threats.[^cite_note-track-425]

In 1977, district court judge [John Lewis Smith Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis_Smith_Jr.) ordered the recorded audiotapes and written transcripts resulting from the FBI's electronic surveillance of King between 1963-68 to be sealed from public access in the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_and_Records_Administration) until 2027.[^cite_note-450] In 2019, an FBI file emerged on which a handwritten note alleged that King "looked on, laughed and offered advice" as one of his friends raped a woman. Historians who have examined this notional evidence have dismissed it as highly unreliable,[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-Mur19-452] as an uncorroborated FBI report during a period when it was undertaking an operation to attempt to discredit King.[^cite_note-453] The tapes that could confirm or refute the allegation are scheduled to be declassified in 2027.[^cite_note-454]

In his 1989 autobiography *[And the Walls Came Tumbling Down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/And_the_Walls_Came_Tumbling_Down)*, Ralph Abernathy stated that King had a "weakness for women", although they "all understood and believed in the biblical prohibition against sex outside of marriage. It was just that he had a particularly difficult time with that temptation."[^cite_note-455] In a later interview, Abernathy said he only wrote the term "womanizing", that he did not specifically say King had [extramarital sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extramarital_sex) and that the infidelities were [emotional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotional_affair) rather than sexual.[^cite_note-abertappva-456] Abernathy criticized the media for sensationalizing the statements he wrote about King's affairs,[^cite_note-abertappva-456] such as the allegation King had a sexual affair the night before he was assassinated.[^cite_note-abertappva-456] In his 1986 book *[Bearing the Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bearing_the_Cross)*, Garrow wrote about affairs, including one woman King saw almost daily. According to Garrow, "that relationship ... increasingly became the emotional centerpiece of King's life, but it did not eliminate the incidental couplings ... of King's travels." He alleged that King explained his affairs as "a form of anxiety reduction". Garrow asserted that King's supposed promiscuity caused him "painful and at times overwhelming guilt".[^cite_note-457] King's wife, Coretta, appeared to have accepted his affairs with equanimity, saying once that "all that other business just doesn't have a place in the very high-level relationship we enjoyed."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrady200267-458] Shortly after *Bearing the Cross* was released, civil rights author [Howell Raines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howell_Raines) gave the book a positive review but opined that Garrow's allegations about King's sex life were "sensational" and stated that Garrow was "amassing facts rather than analyzing them".[^cite_note-459]

## Awards and recognition



King showing his medallion, which he received from Mayor Wagner, 1964

King was awarded at least fifty honorary degrees from colleges and universities.[^cite_note-merv-460] On October 14, 1964, King became the youngest winner of the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md), which was awarded to him for leading nonviolent resistance to racial prejudice in the U.S.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462] In 1965, he was awarded the American Liberties Medallion by the [American Jewish Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jewish_Committee) for his "exceptional advancement of the principles of human liberty."[^cite_note-merv-460][^cite_note-463] In his acceptance remarks, King said, "Freedom is one thing. You have it all or you are not free."[^cite_note-464]

In 1957, he was awarded the [Spingarn Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spingarn_Medal) from the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP).[^cite_note-465] Two years later, he won the [Anisfield-Wolf Book Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anisfield-Wolf_Book_Award) for *Stride Toward Freedom: The Montgomery Story*.[^cite_note-466] In 1966, the Planned Parenthood Federation of America awarded King the [Margaret Sanger Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Sanger_Awards) for "his courageous resistance to bigotry and his lifelong dedication to the advancement of social justice and human dignity."[^cite_note-PP-467] Also in 1966, King was elected as a fellow of the [American Academy of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Arts_and_Sciences).[^cite_note-King_AAAS_fellow-468] In November 1967, he made a 24-hour trip to the UK to receive an honorary [Doctorate in Civil Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Civil_Law) from [Newcastle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_University), becoming the first African American the institution had recognized in this way.[^cite_note-Ward-318] In an impromptu acceptance speech,[^cite_note-Newcastle_ceremony-317] he said:

> There are three urgent and indeed great problems that we face not only in the United States of America but all over the world today. That is the problem of racism, the problem of poverty and the problem of war.



King after receiving his honorary doctorate from [Newcastle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_University)

In addition to his nominations for three Grammy Awards, King posthumously won for [Best Spoken Word Recording](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Spoken_Word_Album) in 1971 for "Why I Oppose The War In Vietnam".[^cite_note-469]

In 1977, President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) posthumously awarded the [Presidential Medal of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom) to King. The citation read:

> Martin Luther King Jr. was the conscience of his generation. He gazed upon the great wall of segregation and saw that the power of love could bring it down. From the pain and exhaustion of his fight to fulfill the promises of our founding fathers for our humblest citizens, he wrung his eloquent statement of his dream for America. He made our nation stronger because he made it better. His dream sustains us yet.[^cite_note-470]

King and his wife were also awarded the [Congressional Gold Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Gold_Medal) in 2004.[^cite_note-471]

King was second in [Gallup's List of Most Widely Admired People of the 20th Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup's_List_of_Most_Widely_Admired_People_of_the_20th_Century).[^cite_note-472] In 1963, he was named [*Time* Person of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Person_of_the_Year), and, in 2000, he was voted sixth in an online "Person of the Century" poll by the same magazine.[^cite_note-473] King placed third in *[The Greatest American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Greatest_American)* conducted by the [Discovery Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Channel) and [AOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AOL).[^cite_note-474]

### Five-dollar bill

On April 20, 2016, Treasury Secretary [Jacob Lew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Lew) announced that the \$5, \$10, and \$20 bills would all undergo redesign prior to 2020. Lew said that while Lincoln would remain on the front of the \$5 bill, the reverse would be redesigned to depict various historical events that had occurred at the Lincoln Memorial. Among the planned designs are images from King's "I Have a Dream" speech.[^cite_note-475]

### Memorials

Main article: [List of memorials to Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_memorials_to_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.)

Many memorial sites, buildings and sculptures have been created to honor Martin Luther King Jr, including the [Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Memorial_Library) in Washington, D.C.,[^cite_note-476] the [Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Library) in [San Jose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose,_California), California, and the [Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Memorial) in [West Potomac Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Potomac_Park) next to the [National Mall](167796.md) in Washington, D.C.

### Honorary doctorates

King has received several [honorary doctorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorary_doctorates).[^cite_note-477]

- 1957: [Doctor of Humane Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Humane_Letters), [Morehouse College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morehouse_College); [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [Howard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_University); [Doctor of Divinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Divinity), [Chicago Theological Seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Theological_Seminary)

- 1961: [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [Lincoln University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_University_\(Pennsylvania\)); [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [University of Bridgeport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bridgeport)

- 1964: [Doctor of Divinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Divinity), [Wesleyan College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wesleyan_College); [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [Jewish Theological Seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Theological_Seminary_of_America); [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [Yale University](34273.md); [Doctor of Divinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Divinity), [Springfield College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield_College)

- 1965: [Doctor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws), [Hofstra University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hofstra_University); [Doctor of Humane Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Humane_Letters), [Oberlin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oberlin_College); [Doctor of Social Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Social_Science), [Amsterdam Free University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amsterdam_Free_University); [Doctor of Divinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Divinity), [St. Peter's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter's_University)
